Current Position
Research Fellow — Physics Institute (PHI) & Institute of Quantum Materials and Technologies (IQMT), Karlsruhe Institute of Technology (KIT), Germany — since Aug 2025
Research Focus

Instrumentation & methodology for molecular electronics

Designing novel methods to explore charge transport across metal(carbon) │ molecule (or SAM) │ metal(carbon) junctions under external bias or temperature gradient, and evaluating how transport through single molecules and hybrid assemblies is modulated by external stimuli — light, magnetic field, and chemical gating. At KIT, this work centers on a liquid-helium-temperature scanning tunnelling microscope, developing advanced instrumentation to study light–matter interactions and quantum transport at the nanoscale.

Technical Expertise & Research Skills

During his PhD, Umar independently built specialized lab equipment, including an EGaIn-based system for studying molecular junctions and STM / MCBJ break-junction setups for measuring electrical properties at the nanoscale, designed using AutoCAD Inventor. He developed and 3D-printed multiple prototypes before converging on the most stable designs, and wrote custom LabVIEW software to control the instruments and automate data analysis — including algorithms to extract meaningful correlations and key metrics from noisy data, work that has since been published. While LabVIEW remains his primary tool, he is comfortable moving to Python for future projects. He is also familiar with quantum-mechanical calculations from his master's work, mainly using Gaussian with some early work in SIESTA, though transport calculations were outside the scope of his PhD, which focused on experiment. His chemistry background spans UV-Vis and fluorescence spectroscopy, atomic force microscopy, electrochemical methods, and early-stage synthesis, complemented by an undergraduate foundation in biology and biochemistry. Overall, he combines hands-on engineering — building instruments and writing control software — with depth across chemistry and biology, allowing him to work across materials science and the life sciences.
